We first control a square function of the exact prime supports, then recover the sum by symmetrization. For related arithmetic moment estimates and Fourier lifting, see (Bloom and Maynard 2022, Theorem 2 of the arXiv version) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[BloomMaynard2022](#ref-BloomMaynard2022)] and (Green and Sawhney 2025, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[GreenSawhney2025](#ref-GreenSawhney2025)]; the uniform estimate used here is proved below.

Let $\mathcal P$ be a finite set of primes, and give $$X_{\mathcal P}=\prod_{p\in\mathcal P}\mathbb F_p$$ uniform probability measure. A character is indexed by $\xi=\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}a_p/p\pmod1$, where $a_p\in\{0,\ldots,p-1\}$, and we write $$e(z\xi)=\prod_{p\in\mathcal P}e(a_pz_p/p),
 \qquad e(t)=\exp(2\pi i t).$$ Its exact prime support is $\{p:a_p\ne0\}$, and its reduced denominator is the product of these primes. In particular $q(0)=1$. Summing a geometric progression in each coordinate shows that these characters form an orthonormal basis. For $U\subseteq\mathcal P$, put $q_U=\prod_{p\in U}p$, with $q_\varnothing=1$.

Let $I$ be a nonempty consecutive interval of integers, of length $L$, and let $f:I\to\mathbb C$. Define $$\widehat f_I(\xi)=\frac1L\sum_{n\in I}f(n)e(-n\xi),
 \qquad
 f_U(z_U)=\sum_{q(\xi)=q_U}\widehat f_I(\xi)e(z_U\xi).$$ For $Q\ge1$, the Fourier lift is $$\mathcal L_{I,\mathcal P,Q}f
   =\sum_{\substack{U\subseteq\mathcal P\\q_U\le Q}}f_U.$$ If $\mathcal F$ is a subfamily of these supports, write $\mathcal L^{\mathcal F}_{I,\mathcal P,Q}f=\sum_{U\in\mathcal F}f_U$. Thus retaining a support means retaining all frequencies of its exact denominator. Each $f_U$ depends only on $z_U$ and has mean zero in each coordinate belonging to $U$. Real $f$ gives real $f_U$, because the summation set is closed under negation.

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-2"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 14* (A lift need not be nonnegative). Take distinct primes $p,q$ with $p,q\le Q<pq$ and $\mathcal P=\{p,q\}$. Let $I$ have length divisible by $pq$, and let $f$ be the indicator of the simultaneous congruences $n\equiv a\pmod p$ and $n\equiv b\pmod q$. The cutoff retains the supports $\varnothing,\{p\},\{q\}$ and omits $\{p,q\}$. Since each residue pair occurs $|I|/(pq)$ times, summing these three components gives $$\mathcal L_{I,\mathcal P,Q}f(z_p,z_q)
 =\frac{1_{z_p=a}}q+\frac{1_{z_q=b}}p-\frac1{pq}.$$ In particular, the lift equals $-1/(pq)$ when both coordinates miss the prescribed residues. This is why the multilinear estimates must allow signed inputs even when the original function is an indicator.

</div>

<a id="stmt-lem-lift-moments"></a>
<div id="lem:lift-moments" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:lift-explicit-moment"></a>
**Lemma 15** (Uniform moments of Fourier lifts). *Let $Q\ge1$, let $I$ have length $L\ge10Q^6$, and suppose $\|f\|_\infty\le M$. Let $\mathcal F$ be any subfamily of $\{U\subseteq\mathcal P:q_U\le Q\}$, and put $$k=\max\{|U|:U\subseteq\mathcal P,\ q_U\le Q\}.$$ For every integer $r\ge1$, define $$K_{2r}=\left(\frac{(2r)!}{2^r r!}\right)^{1/(2r)},
 \qquad
 A_r=2eK_{2r}\,2^{(r+3)/4}.$$ Then $$\left\|\mathcal L^{\mathcal F}_{I,\mathcal P,Q}f\right\|_{2r}
 \le 2M(k+1)A_r^k.
 \label{eq:lift-explicit-moment}$$ Consequently, for fixed $r$, this norm is at most $M Q^{o_r(1)}$ as $Q\to\infty$, uniformly in $I,f,\mathcal P$, and $\mathcal F$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-lift-moments"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-lift-moments</summary>

<a id="eq:lift-core-bound"></a>
*Proof.* *Conditional square-sum bound.* We establish a conditional square-sum bound before estimating moments. For every $B\subseteq\mathcal P$ with $q_B\le Q$, and every fixed value $z_B$, we claim that $$\sum_{\substack{U\in\mathcal F\\U\supseteq B}}
   \mathbb E\bigl(|f_U|^2\mid z_B\bigr)
 \le 4M^2 4^{|B|}.
 \label{eq:lift-core-bound}$$ The conditional expectation here integrates the coordinates outside $B$; those outside $U$ are irrelevant to its summand.

<a id="eq:lift-core-coefficient"></a>
To compute its coefficients, write $U=B\sqcup A$ and uniquely decompose a frequency on $U$ as $\xi=\xi_B+\eta$, with exact supports $B$ and $A$, respectively. After fixing $z_B$, the coefficient of the outside character $e(z_A\eta)$ is $$c_B(\eta;z_B)=\frac1L\sum_{n\in I}f(n)e(-n\eta)
       \prod_{p\in B}\bigl(p1_{n\equiv z_p\ (p)}-1\bigr).
 \label{eq:lift-core-coefficient}$$ Indeed, the sum over the nonzero frequencies at $p$ is $$\sum_{a=1}^{p-1}e\bigl(a(z_p-n)/p\bigr)
   =p1_{n\equiv z_p\ (p)}-1.$$ Let $\Omega_B$ denote the union of the outside-frequency sets arising from $U\in\mathcal F$ with $U\supseteq B$. Different outside supports give disjoint frequency sets, and all their denominators are at most $Q/q_B$ and coprime to $q_B$. Conditional Parseval therefore identifies the left side of <a href="#eq:lift-core-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift-core-bound">[eq:lift-core-bound]</a> with $\sum_{\eta\in\Omega_B}|c_B(\eta;z_B)|^2$.

<a id="eq:lift-progression-coefficient"></a>
Expand the product in <a href="#eq:lift-core-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift-core-coefficient">[eq:lift-core-coefficient]</a> by subsets $J\subseteq B$. Apart from its sign, the corresponding coefficient vector is $$c_J(\eta)=\frac{q_J}{L}
   \sum_{\substack{n\in I\\n\equiv z_J\ (q_J)}}f(n)e(-n\eta),
 \qquad \eta\in\Omega_B.
 \label{eq:lift-progression-coefficient}$$ The congruence in this expression is the simultaneous congruence at the primes of $J$, or equivalently its unique class modulo $q_J$. For $J=\varnothing$ it imposes no restriction.

Write its progression as $n=a+q_Jt$, where $t$ ranges over a consecutive interval $T$ of length $L_J$. Since $q_J\le Q$ and $L\ge10Q^6$, $$|L_J-L/q_J|\le1,
 \qquad L_J\ge0.9L/q_J,
 \qquad q_JL_J/L\le1.1.$$ After removing the phase $e(-a\eta)$, <a href="#eq:lift-progression-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift-progression-coefficient">[eq:lift-progression-coefficient]</a> is $q_JL_J/L$ times the normalized Fourier analysis of $t\mapsto f(a+q_Jt)$ at frequencies $q_J\eta$.

These frequencies remain distinct. In fact the denominator of $\eta-\eta'$ is coprime to $q_J$, so $q_J(\eta-\eta')\in\mathbb Z$ implies $\eta=\eta'\pmod1$. Multiplication by $q_J$ also preserves each reduced outside denominator. Thus all the resulting frequencies have denominators at most $Q$, are pairwise separated in circle distance by at least $Q^{-2}$, and number at most $\sum_{q\le Q}\varphi(q)\le Q^2$, where $\varphi(1)=1$ accounts for the zero frequency.

This is a separated-frequency estimate of large-sieve type; compare (Montgomery and Vaughan 1973, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[MontgomeryVaughan1973](#ref-MontgomeryVaughan1973)]. The length hypothesis permits the following elementary Gram-matrix bound. Consider the Gram matrix of these normalized exponential vectors in $L^2(T)$. Its diagonal is 1. For distinct frequencies $\theta,\theta'$, geometric summation gives $$\left|\frac1{L_J}\sum_{t\in T}e\bigl(t(\theta-\theta')\bigr)\right|
 \le \frac1{2L_J\|\theta-\theta'\|_{\mathbb R/\mathbb Z}}
 \le\frac{Q^2}{2L_J}.$$ Its operator norm is at most its maximum absolute row sum, hence at most $$1+\frac{Q^4}{2L_J}
 \le1+\frac{Q^4q_J}{1.8L}
 \le1+\frac1{18Q}\le\frac{19}{18}.$$ The norm squared of the Fourier analysis map equals this Gram-matrix norm. Consequently $$\left(\sum_{\eta\in\Omega_B}|c_J(\eta)|^2\right)^{1/2}
 \le1.1\sqrt{19/18}\,
       \left(\frac1{L_J}\sum_{t\in T}|f(a+q_Jt)|^2\right)^{1/2}
 \le2M.$$ The Euclidean triangle inequality over the $2^{|B|}$ subsets $J$ proves <a href="#eq:lift-core-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift-core-bound">[eq:lift-core-bound]</a>.

<a id="eq:lift-square-function"></a>
*Square-function moments.* We next use this bound to control $$S(z)=\left(\sum_{U\in\mathcal F}|f_U(z_U)|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ Put $T_r=\mathbb E S^{2r}$ and $T_0=1$. Expand the first $r-1$ factors in this moment and fix their supports $U_1,\ldots,U_{r-1}$. Their product depends only on coordinates in $W=U_1\cup\cdots\cup U_{r-1}$, and $|W|\le(r-1)k$. For a last support $U$, let $B=U\cap W$. Integrating outside $W$ gives exactly $\mathbb E(|f_U|^2\mid z_B)$, because the coordinates of $f_U$ outside $B$ are fresh. For each fixed intersection $B$, its sum over $U\cap W=B$ is bounded by <a href="#eq:lift-core-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift-core-bound">[eq:lift-core-bound]</a>. Only $B$ with $q_B\le Q$ can occur, and there are at most $2^{(r-1)k}$ possible intersections. It follows that $$T_r\le4M^2 4^k2^{(r-1)k}T_{r-1}.$$ Induction gives $$T_r\le(4M^2)^r4^{kr}2^{kr(r-1)/2},
 \qquad
 \|S\|_{2r}\le2M\,2^{k(r+3)/4}.
 \label{eq:lift-square-function}$$ This conditioning argument permits arbitrary overlaps among supports. The remaining task is to recover their sum from this square function.

*Recovering the sum.* Fix a size $j\ge1$ and color the coordinates independently and uniformly with $j$ colors. A support is called transversal if it contains exactly one coordinate of each color. Each support of size $j$ is transversal with probability $\pi_j=j!/j^j$. Fix one coloring $c$, and let $\mathcal T$ be its retained transversal supports in $\mathcal F$.

<a id="eq:lift-sign-symmetrization"></a>
Take independent copies $X_p^0,X_p^1$ of every coordinate. Write $\operatorname{swap}_p$ for interchanging that pair, and define $$D_U=\prod_{p\in U}(I-\operatorname{swap}_p)f_U(X^0).$$ Coordinatewise centering gives $\mathbb E_{X^1}D_U=f_U(X^0)$: in the expanded product, every term using at least one copied coordinate has zero conditional mean. Jensen’s inequality therefore gives $$\left\|\sum_{U\in\mathcal T}f_U\right\|_{2r}
 \le\left\|\sum_{U\in\mathcal T}D_U\right\|_{L^{2r}(X^0,X^1)}.$$ Swapping independent copies coordinate by coordinate is a standard symmetrization device; see (Peña and Montgomery-Smith 1995, sec. 4 of the arXiv version) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[DeLaPenaMontgomerySmith1995](#ref-DeLaPenaMontgomerySmith1995)]. Independently swapping each coordinate pair preserves their joint law and multiplies $D_U$ by the product of the associated signs. With independent random signs $(\epsilon_p)_{p\in\mathcal P}$, $$\left\|\sum_{U\in\mathcal T}D_U\right\|_{L^{2r}(X^0,X^1)}
 =\left\|\sum_{U\in\mathcal T}D_U\prod_{p\in U}\epsilon_p
       \right\|_{L^{2r}(X^0,X^1,\epsilon)}.
 \label{eq:lift-sign-symmetrization}$$ The signs here are indexed by individual coordinates, not merely by colors. Each monomial contains one coordinate sign from each color class.

<a id="eq:lift-scalar-khintchine"></a>
For completeness, the scalar random-sign bound with the constant $K_{2r}$ above is $$\left\|\sum_i a_i\epsilon_i\right\|_{2r}
 \le K_{2r}\left(\sum_i|a_i|^2\right)^{1/2}.
 \label{eq:lift-scalar-khintchine}$$ For nonnegative coefficients $b_i$, expansion of the even moment retains only multiplicities $2m_i$ with $\sum_i m_i=r$. Since $(2m)!\ge2^m m!$, that moment is at most $$\frac{(2r)!}{2^r}
 \sum_{\sum m_i=r}\prod_i\frac{b_i^{2m_i}}{m_i!}
 =\frac{(2r)!}{2^r r!}\left(\sum_i b_i^2\right)^r.$$ For complex $a_i$, expand $(\sum a_i\epsilon_i)^r(\sum\overline{a_i}\epsilon_i)^r$ and take absolute values termwise; its surviving terms are bounded by the expansion with $b_i=|a_i|$. This proves <a href="#eq:lift-scalar-khintchine" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift-scalar-khintchine">[eq:lift-scalar-khintchine]</a> in the required generality.

For fixed copies $X^0,X^1$, iterate <a href="#eq:lift-scalar-khintchine" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift-scalar-khintchine">[eq:lift-scalar-khintchine]</a> through the $j$ color classes. At each stage one uses Minkowski in $L^r$, in the form $$\left\|\left(\sum_i|B_i|^2\right)^{1/2}\right\|_{2r}
 \le\left(\sum_i\|B_i\|_{2r}^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ Induction on the number of color classes therefore gives $$\left\|\sum_{U\in\mathcal T}D_U\prod_{p\in U}\epsilon_p
       \right\|_{L^{2r}(\epsilon)}
 \le K_{2r}^j\left(\sum_{U\in\mathcal T}|D_U|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$

To estimate this difference square function, index the choices of copies by $\eta\in\{0,1\}^j$, one choice for each color. Since each $U\in\mathcal T$ has one coordinate of every color, $$D_U=\sum_{\eta\in\{0,1\}^j}(-1)^{|\eta|}
    f_U\bigl((X_p^{\eta_{c(p)}})_{p\in U}\bigr).$$ For each fixed $\eta$, the selected full coordinate vector has the original product law. Minkowski in $L^{2r}(\ell^2(\mathcal T))$ consequently yields $$\left\|\left(\sum_{U\in\mathcal T}|D_U|^2\right)^{1/2}\right\|_{2r}
 \le2^j\left\|\left(\sum_{U\in\mathcal T}|f_U|^2\right)^{1/2}\right\|_{2r}
 \le2^j\|S\|_{2r}.$$ Together with <a href="#eq:lift-sign-symmetrization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift-sign-symmetrization">[eq:lift-sign-symmetrization]</a>, this proves the transversal bound $(2K_{2r})^j\|S\|_{2r}$.

Average over colorings. Since every support is retained with probability $\pi_j$, another application of Minkowski gives $$\left\|\sum_{\substack{U\in\mathcal F\\|U|=j}}f_U\right\|_{2r}
 \le\frac{j^j}{j!}(2K_{2r})^j\|S\|_{2r}
 \le(2eK_{2r})^j\|S\|_{2r}.$$ Here $j!\ge(j/e)^j$, which also follows by integrating $\log x$ from 1 to $j$. The term of size zero is constant and bounded in absolute value by $S$. Summing over $0\le j\le k$ and using <a href="#eq:lift-square-function" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift-square-function">[eq:lift-square-function]</a> proves <a href="#eq:lift-explicit-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift-explicit-moment">[eq:lift-explicit-moment]</a>.

Finally, the product of $k$ distinct primes is at least $(k+1)!$, so $(k+1)!\le Q$. The bound $\log(k!)\ge k\log k-k$ gives $k=O(\log Q/\log\log Q)$. For fixed $r$, the logarithm of the right side of <a href="#eq:lift-explicit-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift-explicit-moment">[eq:lift-explicit-moment]</a>, after removing $M$, is therefore $o(\log Q)$. Every bound used above was independent of the interval’s location, the ambient prime set, and the retained family. This proves the asserted uniformity. ◻

</details>

We record separately the cost of fixing residue coordinates. This cost is a power of the size of the fixed fiber, and must remain explicit when the moment bound is used in multilinear estimates.

<a id="stmt-lem-lift-specialization"></a>
<div id="lem:lift-specialization" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:lift-specialization"></a>
**Lemma 16** (Specialization of lifts). *Let $B\subseteq\mathcal P$, let $z_B\in X_B$, and let $G:X_{\mathcal P}\to\mathbb C$. For every finite $p\ge1$, $$\|G(z_B,\cdot)\|_{L^p(X_{\mathcal P\setminus B})}
 \le q_B^{1/p}\|G\|_{L^p(X_{\mathcal P})}.
 \label{eq:lift-specialization}$$ In the setting of Lemma <a href="#lem:lift-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lift-moments">15</a>, this gives, for every fixed $z_B$, $$\left\|\bigl(\mathcal L^{\mathcal F}_{I,\mathcal P,Q}f\bigr)
                  (z_B,\cdot)\right\|_{2r}
 \le 2M q_B^{1/(2r)}(k+1)A_r^k.$$ The same bound holds for either part obtained by cutting the remaining denominator at any $H\ge1$: before specialization these parts correspond to the whole-support families $$\mathcal F_{\le H,B}
  =\{U\in\mathcal F:q_{U\setminus B}\le H\},
 \qquad
 \mathcal F_{>H,B}
  =\{U\in\mathcal F:q_{U\setminus B}>H\}.$$ After specialization their Fourier supports lie, respectively, at remaining denominators at most $H$ and greater than $H$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-lift-specialization"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-lift-specialization</summary>

*Proof.* The specified fiber has uniform probability $1/q_B$. Its contribution to $\mathbb E|G|^p$ is $$q_B^{-1}\mathbb E_{z_{\mathcal P\setminus B}}
 |G(z_B,z_{\mathcal P\setminus B})|^p.$$ Discarding all other nonnegative contributions proves <a href="#eq:lift-specialization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift-specialization">[eq:lift-specialization]</a>. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:lift-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lift-moments">15</a> to each displayed family. Every character of $f_U$, when specialized on $B$, retains exactly the coordinate support $U\setminus B$. Combining such characters can cancel coefficients but cannot create frequencies outside the asserted remaining-denominator ranges. This proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

In particular, for $d\ge2$, a multilinear estimate using one $L^2$ norm and $d-1$ norms in $L^{2(d-1)}$, with the same coordinates fixed in every slot, incurs the total specialization factor $$q_B^{1/2}\left(q_B^{1/(2(d-1))}\right)^{d-1}=q_B.$$ Two specialized $L^2$ factors likewise incur $q_B$. Multiplying the original bounded function by a fixed constant simply multiplies every norm bound by the absolute value of that constant.

For later uniform estimates one can make the subpower dependence entirely explicit. Put $$k_*(Q)=\max\{j\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}:(j+1)!\le Q\},
 \qquad
 \mathfrak M_r(Q)=2\bigl(k_*(Q)+1\bigr)A_r^{k_*(Q)}.$$ The moment bound is at most $M\mathfrak M_r(Q)$, and for every fixed $r$ and $\epsilon>0$, one has $\mathfrak M_r(Q)\le Q^\epsilon$ for sufficiently large $Q$. The threshold may depend on $r,\epsilon$, but on none of the intervals, functions, fibers, or retained families. Thus any fixed finite product of the envelopes $\mathfrak M_r(Q)$ remains subpower, even when their fixed moment orders are very large. The displayed input bounds and specialization powers remain explicit.

<a id="sec:square-kernel"></a>
### A signed kernel for actual square differences

The preceding tuple measures impose square-difference constraints in residue fields. This section supplies the passage to actual positive integer squares. We construct a signed measure supported on such squares and approximate its Fourier transform uniformly by a sum over small rational frequencies. Because every actual square shift vanishes between the supports under consideration, the signs of the measure cause no difficulty. The weighted square sums and major/minor-arc analysis have a direct antecedent in (Green and Sawhney 2025, sec. 5) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[GreenSawhney2025](#ref-GreenSawhney2025)]. Here the signed divisor weight is chosen to match the strict-square pair law of our finite-field tuples.

<a id="eq:kernel-beta"></a>
For the remainder of the paper, fix $$\beta=10^{-5}.
 \label{eq:kernel-beta}$$ Write $e(t)=\exp(2\pi i t)$, and let $q(\xi)$ be the reduced denominator of $\xi\in\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z$, with $q(0)=1$. For every rational frequency define the normalized unit quadratic sum <a id="eq:kernel-unit-gauss"></a>$$\mathfrak g(\xi)
 =\frac{1}{\varphi(q(\xi))}
   \sum_{m\in(\mathbb Z/q(\xi)\mathbb Z)^\times}e(\xi m^2),
 \qquad \mathfrak g(0)=1.
 \label{eq:kernel-unit-gauss}$$ Here $\varphi$ is Euler’s totient function. If $D=8d_0$ with $d_0$ odd and squarefree, call a residue modulo $D$ a *strict square class* if it is $1$ modulo $8$ and a nonzero square modulo every prime dividing $d_0$.

<a id="stmt-prop-square-kernel"></a>
<div id="prop:square-kernel" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:kernel-conclusion"></a>
**Proposition 17** (Ordered square-kernel estimate). *Let $N\ge1$ be an integer, put $H=N^{\beta/2}$, and let $D=8d_0\le N^\beta$, where $d_0$ is odd and squarefree. Suppose $F,J:[N]\to\mathbb C$ satisfy $|F|,|J|\le1$ and are supported in residue classes $a,b$ modulo $D$, respectively. Assume that $b-a$ is a strict square class, that $x<y$ whenever $x\in\operatorname{supp}F$ and $y\in\operatorname{supp}J$, and that no such difference $y-x$ is the square of a positive integer. With $$\widehat F(\xi)=\frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N F(n)e(-n\xi),
 \qquad
 \widehat J(\xi)=\frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N J(n)e(-n\xi),$$ one has $$D\left|
 \sum_{\substack{\xi\in\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z:\ q(\xi)\le H\\
                  q(\xi)\ \mathrm{squarefree},\ (q(\xi),D)=1}}
      \mathfrak g(\xi)\widehat F(-\xi)\widehat J(\xi)
 \right|
 \ll H^{-1/3}.
 \label{eq:kernel-conclusion}$$ The implied constant is absolute and is independent of all the data in the statement.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-square-kernel"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-square-kernel</summary>

*Proof.* Put $r=b-a$ modulo $D$, and denote the frequency set in <a href="#eq:kernel-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-conclusion">[eq:kernel-conclusion]</a> by $\Xi$. We first define the model and actual-square kernels. We then compare their Fourier transforms on minor and major arcs, and finish with the exact bilinear Fourier identity.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
###### The two kernels.

<a id="eq:kernel-sieve-weight"></a>
For every odd prime $p\mid d_0$, choose one root $c_p$ of $r$ modulo $p$. The conditions $m\equiv c_p\pmod p$ for all such primes, together with $m$ odd, specify one residue class modulo $D'=2d_0=D/4$. Let $\rho_D$ be $D'$ times the indicator of this class. Thus $\rho_D$ has periodic mean one and supremum $D'$. For every odd prime $p\nmid D$, set $$w_p(m)=\frac{p\mathbf1_{p\mid m}-1}{p-1}
       =\frac1{p-1}\sum_{j=1}^{p-1}e(jm/p),
 \qquad
 w_u(m)=\prod_{p\mid u}w_p(m),
 \label{eq:kernel-sieve-weight}$$ where $u$ is squarefree and coprime to $D$, and $w_1=1$. The second equality follows by summing all characters modulo $p$. In particular $|w_u|\le1$.

<a id="eq:kernel-complete-sieve"></a>
The factor $1-w_p(m)=p(p-1)^{-1}1_{p\nmid m}$ is the density of the uniform unit law modulo $p$ relative to the uniform law. Hence for any positive integer $q$ coprime to $D$, $$\sum_{u\mid\operatorname{rad}(q)}(-1)^{\omega(u)}w_u(m)
 =\prod_{p\mid q}(1-w_p(m))
 =\frac q{\varphi(q)}1_{(m,q)=1}.
 \label{eq:kernel-complete-sieve}$$ Here $\omega(u)$ counts the distinct prime divisors of $u$, and $\operatorname{rad}(q)$ is the product of the distinct primes dividing $q$. Thus the complete divisor sum converts a uniform residue into a uniform unit, whose square has Fourier multiplier $\mathfrak g$. We truncate this sum at $u\le H$ in the actual-square kernel. At a rational frequency with denominator $q\le H$ coprime to $D$, all divisors of $\operatorname{rad}(q)$ remain present; the periodic mean of every term containing a prime outside $q$ vanishes. This will give the exact low-denominator comparison. The other rational coefficients will be bounded by quadratic Gauss sums.

<a id="eq:kernel-model"></a>
<a id="eq:kernel-actual"></a>
<a id="eq:kernel-uniform-approximation"></a>
Define two finitely supported functions on $\mathbb Z$ by $$\begin{aligned}
 k_0(n)
 &=\frac DN\mathbf1_{1\le n\le N}\mathbf1_{n\equiv r\ (D)}
       \sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\mathfrak g(\xi)e(-\xi n),
       \label{eq:kernel-model}\\
 k(n)
 &=\sum_{1\le m\le\sqrt N}\mathbf1_{n=m^2}\frac{2m}{N}\rho_D(m)
       \sum_{\substack{u\le H\ \mathrm{squarefree}\\(u,D)=1}}
                   (-1)^{\omega(u)}w_u(m).
       \label{eq:kernel-actual}
\end{aligned}$$ The first kernel represents the rational expression in the proposition; the second is supported on actual positive integer squares. The factor $2m/N$ is the discrete counterpart of the substitution $dn=2m\,dm$, matching the uniform weight $1/N$ in the model kernel. For these kernels use the positive-sign Fourier transforms $$S_0(\alpha)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb Z}k_0(n)e(\alpha n),
 \qquad
 S(\alpha)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb Z}k(n)e(\alpha n),
 \qquad \alpha\in\mathbb R/\mathbb Z.$$ Our immediate goal is $$\sup_{\alpha\in\mathbb R/\mathbb Z}|S(\alpha)-S_0(\alpha)|
 \ll H^{-1/3}.
 \label{eq:kernel-uniform-approximation}$$

<a id="eq:kernel-model-centers"></a>
Put $V_N(t)=N^{-1}\sum_{n=1}^N e(tn)$. Expanding the congruence indicator in <a href="#eq:kernel-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-model">[eq:kernel-model]</a> gives $$S_0(\alpha)=\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\sum_{j\bmod D}
    \mathfrak g(\xi)e(-jr/D)V_N(\alpha-\xi+j/D).
 \label{eq:kernel-model-centers}$$ There are at most $DH^2$ terms, each of coefficient modulus at most one, and their centers have denominators at most $DH$. The centers are distinct. Indeed, an equality $\xi-j/D=\xi'-j'/D$ would make $\xi-\xi'$ have denominator both coprime to $D$ and dividing $D$, so that $\xi=\xi'$ and $j=j'$ modulo $D$.

<a id="eq:kernel-model-coefficient"></a>
Every $\alpha_0\in\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z$ has a unique decomposition $\alpha_0=\alpha_D+\alpha_1$ in which the denominator of $\alpha_D$ has only prime factors dividing $D$ and $q_1=q(\alpha_1)$ is coprime to $D$. Thus the coefficient of the center $\alpha_0$ in <a href="#eq:kernel-model-centers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-model-centers">[eq:kernel-model-centers]</a>, taken to be zero when it is absent, is $$c_0(\alpha_0)=
 \begin{cases}
 e(\alpha_Dr)\mathfrak g(\alpha_1),&
 q(\alpha_D)\mid D,\quad q_1\le H,\quad q_1\text{ squarefree},\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:kernel-model-coefficient}$$ The sign is worth recording: in <a href="#eq:kernel-model-centers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-model-centers">[eq:kernel-model-centers]</a>, $\alpha_D=-j/D$, and its coefficient contains $e(-jr/D)$.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
###### Minor arcs.

We initially assume $N$ is sufficiently large by an absolute amount. Take the major arcs to be the closed balls of radius $2N^{-9/10}$ about all reduced rationals of denominator at most $N^{1/10}$. They are disjoint for sufficiently large $N$, since two distinct centers are at least $N^{-1/5}$ apart. The minor arcs are their complement. We write $\|t\|$ for the distance of a real number $t$ to the nearest integer.

<a id="eq:kernel-minor-denominator"></a>
For $\alpha$ on the minor arcs, Dirichlet approximation with $Q_0=\lfloor N^{9/10}\rfloor$ gives coprime integers $a,q$ satisfying $$1\le q\le Q_0,
 \qquad |\alpha-a/q|\le\frac1{qQ_0}\le\frac1{q^2},$$ where the difference is represented by a real number of least absolute value. For completeness, partitioning the circle into $Q_0$ intervals and placing the $Q_0+1$ points $0,\alpha,\ldots,Q_0\alpha$ in them gives integers $1\le h\le Q_0$ and $b$ with $|h\alpha-b|\le Q_0^{-1}$. Reducing $b/h$ to $a/q$ gives the displayed bounds. If $q\le N^{1/10}$, the first approximation bound would put $\alpha$ within $2N^{-9/10}$ of a major-arc center. Consequently $$N^{1/10}<q\le N^{9/10}.
 \label{eq:kernel-minor-denominator}$$

<a id="eq:kernel-differencing"></a>
Let $M=\lfloor\sqrt N\rfloor$. Uniformly in real $\theta$ and integers $1\le L\le M$, differencing and a geometric sum give $$\left|\sum_{m=1}^L e(\alpha m^2+\theta m)\right|^2
 \ll M+\sum_{j=1}^M\min\bigl(M,\|2\alpha j\|^{-1}\bigr).
 \label{eq:kernel-differencing}$$ Indeed, pairs at distance $j$ leave a linear phase with coefficient $2\alpha j$ in the inner sum. Its remaining factors, including $e(\theta j)$, have modulus one. This also proves the asserted uniformity in the linear twist.

<a id="eq:kernel-spacing-sum"></a>
<a id="eq:kernel-minor-quadratic"></a>
Partition the $j$ indices into consecutive blocks of length $\lfloor q/4\rfloor$. Two distinct indices in a block differ by $0<h<q/4$. The residue $2ah$ is nonzero modulo $q$: since $(a,q)=1$, its vanishing would force $q\mid2h$, whereas $0<2h<q/2$. This argument includes even $q$. Hence $$\|2\alpha h\|\ge\|2ah/q\|-2h/q^2\ge\frac1{2q}.$$ In each block the points $2\alpha j$ are therefore separated on the circle by at least $1/(2q)$. Ordering their distances to zero bounds the contribution of one block by $O(M+q\log(2q))$: only a bounded number can lie within distance $1/q$ of zero, and the others contribute a harmonic sum. There are $O(M/q+1)$ blocks, so $$\sum_{j=1}^M\min\bigl(M,\|2\alpha j\|^{-1}\bigr)
 \ll (M/q+1)\bigl(M+q\log(2q)\bigr).
 \label{eq:kernel-spacing-sum}$$ By <a href="#eq:kernel-minor-denominator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-minor-denominator">[eq:kernel-minor-denominator]</a>, <a href="#eq:kernel-differencing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-differencing">[eq:kernel-differencing]</a>–<a href="#eq:kernel-spacing-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-spacing-sum">[eq:kernel-spacing-sum]</a> imply $$\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb R}\ \max_{L\le M}
 \left|\sum_{m=1}^L e(\alpha m^2+\theta m)\right|
 \ll N^{9/20}\sqrt{\log N}.$$ Partial summation with the weight $2m/N$ gives $$\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb R}
 \left|\sum_{m\le\sqrt N}\frac{2m}{N}
                    e(\alpha m^2+\theta m)\right|
 \ll N^{-1/20}\sqrt{\log N}.
 \label{eq:kernel-minor-quadratic}$$

<a id="eq:kernel-minor-actual"></a>
For a periodic function, use Fourier coefficients normalized by its period. The Fourier coefficient $\ell^1$ norm of $\rho_D$ is $D'$: its character expansion has $D'$ coefficients of modulus one. By <a href="#eq:kernel-sieve-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-sieve-weight">[eq:kernel-sieve-weight]</a>, the corresponding norm of each $w_p$ is one, and that of $w_u$ is at most one by the convolution inequality. The entire periodic weight multiplying the quadratic phase in $S$ thus has Fourier coefficient $\ell^1$ norm at most $D'\lfloor H\rfloor\le DH$. Expansion into these characters and <a href="#eq:kernel-minor-quadratic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-minor-quadratic">[eq:kernel-minor-quadratic]</a> yield $$|S(\alpha)|\ll DH N^{-1/20}\sqrt{\log N}
 \qquad(\alpha\text{ on the minor arcs}).
 \label{eq:kernel-minor-actual}$$

<a id="eq:kernel-minor-model"></a>
Every center in <a href="#eq:kernel-model-centers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-model-centers">[eq:kernel-model-centers]</a> has denominator at most $DH\le N^{3\beta/2}<N^{1/10}$, so it is a major-arc center. On the minor arcs its distance from $\alpha$ exceeds $2N^{-9/10}$. The elementary estimate $$|V_N(t)|\le\min\bigl(1,(2N\|t\|)^{-1}\bigr)$$ therefore gives $$|S_0(\alpha)|\ll DH^2N^{-1/10}
 \qquad(\alpha\text{ on the minor arcs}).
 \label{eq:kernel-minor-model}$$

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
###### Reduction on a major arc to periodic means.

<a id="eq:kernel-major-model"></a>
Write $\alpha=\alpha_0+y$, where $q=q(\alpha_0)\le N^{1/10}$ and $|y|\le2N^{-9/10}$. If $\alpha_c\ne\alpha_0$ is a center in <a href="#eq:kernel-model-centers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-model-centers">[eq:kernel-model-centers]</a>, rational separation gives $\|\alpha_c-\alpha_0\|\ge1/(qDH)$. Because $qDH\le N^{1/10+3\beta/2}$, for all sufficiently large $N$ we have $\|\alpha-\alpha_c\|\ge1/(2qDH)$. The contributions of these other centers sum to $$S_0(\alpha)=c_0(\alpha_0)V_N(y)+O(qD^2H^3/N).
 \label{eq:kernel-major-model}$$

<a id="eq:kernel-major-actual"></a>
For an admissible $u$, put $$a_u(m)=\rho_D(m)w_u(m)e(\alpha_0m^2),
 \qquad
 A_u=\frac1{T_u}\sum_{m\bmod T_u}a_u(m),
 \qquad T_u=\operatorname{lcm}(q,D',u).$$ The sequence $a_u$ has period $T_u\le qDu$ and supremum at most $D$. Breaking a partial sum into complete periods and one remaining interval shows, for every real $t\ge0$, that $$\left|\sum_{1\le m\le t}a_u(m)-A_ut\right|\ll DT_u.$$ This includes the fractional part of $t$. For $f_y(x)=(2x/N)e(yx^2)$ on $[0,\sqrt N]$, direct differentiation gives $$|f_y(\sqrt N)|+\int_0^{\sqrt N}|f_y'(x)|\,dx
 \ll\frac{1+|y|N}{\sqrt N}.$$ Stieltjes partial summation consequently gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{m\le\sqrt N}\frac{2m}{N}e(ym^2)a_u(m)
 &=A_u\int_0^{\sqrt N}\frac{2x}{N}e(yx^2)\,dx\\
 &\hspace{5mm}+O\!\left(\frac{qD^2u(1+|y|N)}{\sqrt N}\right).
\end{aligned}$$ The integral equals $N^{-1}\int_0^N e(yt)\,dt$. Comparing the integral to its integer Riemann sum bounds its difference from $V_N(y)$ by $O((1+|y|N)/N)$. Since $|A_u|\le D$, that error is dominated by the preceding displayed error. Summing over $u\le H$ proves $$S(\alpha)=c(\alpha_0)V_N(y)
       +O\!\left(\frac{qD^2H^2(1+|y|N)}{\sqrt N}\right),
 \qquad
 c(\alpha_0)=\sum_{\substack{u\le H\ \mathrm{squarefree}\\(u,D)=1}}
                  (-1)^{\omega(u)}A_u.
 \label{eq:kernel-major-actual}$$ The use of $\sqrt N$ as a real endpoint in this calculation accounts for rounding, while the sum itself contains only integers $m$ with $m^2\le N$.

We have reduced the major-arc comparison to $c(\alpha_0)$ and $c_0(\alpha_0)$. We next compute the contribution at primes dividing $D$, and then the divisor sieve on the coprime denominator $q_1$.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
###### The prescribed roots, including the prime two.

At an odd prime $p\mid D$, the local factor of $\rho_D$ is $p\mathbf1_{m\equiv c_p\ (p)}$. For every $j\ge1$, squaring maps the lifts of $c_p$ modulo $p^j$ bijectively to the residues congruent to $r$ modulo $p$. Indeed, if $x$ is such a lift, then $$(x+tp^k)^2\equiv x^2+2xtp^k\pmod {p^{k+1}},$$ and $2x$ is invertible modulo $p$. This proves the bijection inductively over $k$. For a primitive additive character $e(am^2/p^j)$, its expectation under this normalized root restriction is therefore $e(ar/p)$ if $j=1$, and zero if $j\ge2$: in the latter case one averages a nontrivial character over all the lifts of a single residue modulo $p$. If no $p$-part is present in the character, the expectation is one.

At two the local factor of $\rho_D$ is $2\mathbf1_{m\text{ odd}}$. For $j\le3$, every odd square is $r$ modulo $2^j$, so the expectation of $e(am^2/2^j)$ is $e(ar/2^j)$. For $j\ge3$, the kernel of the squaring homomorphism on $(\mathbb Z/2^j\mathbb Z)^\times$ has exactly four elements. To see this, if $(x-1)(x+1)$ is divisible by $2^j$, exactly one of these two consecutive even factors has 2-adic valuation one, and the other is divisible by $2^{j-1}$. The four solutions modulo $2^j$ are $1,-1,1+2^{j-1},-1+2^{j-1}$. The image of squaring thus has $2^{j-3}$ elements. Every element of this image is $1$ modulo $8$, and this residue class also has $2^{j-3}$ elements. The square of a uniform odd residue modulo $2^j$ is consequently uniform among all residues equal to $1$ modulo $8$. For $j>3$, a primitive character averages to zero on that class.

<a id="eq:kernel-root-law"></a>
Apply these local facts to the decomposition $\alpha_0=\alpha_D+\alpha_1$ already used in <a href="#eq:kernel-model-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-model-coefficient">[eq:kernel-model-coefficient]</a>. Chinese remaindering shows that the factor at primes dividing $D$ is $$\mathbb E_m\rho_D(m)e(\alpha_Dm^2)
 =\begin{cases}
   e(\alpha_Dr),&q(\alpha_D)\mid D,\\
   0,&q(\alpha_D)\nmid D.
  \end{cases}
 \label{eq:kernel-root-law}$$ The expectation here is the uniform average over any common period. The normalized odd-root restrictions, and the normalized oddness restriction at two, are precisely responsible for the factor $D$ in the model kernel. In particular, choosing only one odd root introduces no additional factor in <a href="#eq:kernel-root-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-root-law">[eq:kernel-root-law]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
###### The coprime divisor sieve.

Every prime of an admissible $u$ is coprime to $D$. If such a prime does not divide $q_1$, its mean-zero factor $w_p$ is independent of the exponential and all other factors by the Chinese remainder theorem. Thus $A_u=0$ unless $u\mid\operatorname{rad}(q_1)$.

<a id="eq:kernel-small-coefficient"></a>
Suppose first that $q_1\le H$. All divisors of $\operatorname{rad}(q_1)$ then occur in the sum. By <a href="#eq:kernel-complete-sieve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-complete-sieve">[eq:kernel-complete-sieve]</a>, the divisor weight is the density of the uniform unit law modulo $q_1$ relative to the uniform law. If $p^j$ exactly divides $q_1$ with $j\ge2$, its local primitive unit quadratic average vanishes. Indeed, conditional on a unit $x$ modulo $p^{j-1}$, the $p$ lifts $x+tp^{j-1}$ have phase factors differing by $e(2atx/p)$, where $p\nmid 2ax$. Their sum is zero. If $q_1$ is squarefree, the unit average is exactly $\mathfrak g(\alpha_1)$. Together with <a href="#eq:kernel-root-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-root-law">[eq:kernel-root-law]</a>, this proves $$c(\alpha_0)=c_0(\alpha_0)\qquad(q_1\le H).
 \label{eq:kernel-small-coefficient}$$

<a id="eq:kernel-prime-power-gauss"></a>
<a id="eq:kernel-weighted-prime-power-gauss"></a>
For $q_1>H$ the model coefficient is zero, and we need a bound for the truncated divisor sum. We give the prime-power calculation explicitly. For an odd prime $p$, an integer $j\ge1$, and $p\nmid a$, write $$G_j(a)=\frac1{p^j}\sum_{m\bmod p^j}e(am^2/p^j),
 \qquad G_0(a)=1.$$ Because two is invertible modulo $p^j$, the substitution $(m,n)\mapsto(m-n,m+n)$ is a bijection. Consequently $$|G_j(a)|^2
 =\frac1{p^{2j}}\sum_{u,v\bmod p^j}e(auv/p^j)
 =p^{-j}.
 \label{eq:kernel-prime-power-gauss}$$ Let $T_j=\mathbb E_{m\bmod p^j}\mathbf1_{p\mid m}e(am^2/p^j)$. For $j=1$ one has $T_1=1/p$; for $j\ge2$, substituting $m=pt$ gives $T_j=p^{-1}G_{j-2}(a)$. It follows that $$B_j:=\mathbb E_{m\bmod p^j}w_p(m)e(am^2/p^j)
      =\frac{pT_j-G_j(a)}{p-1},
 \qquad |B_j|\le2p^{-j/2}.
 \label{eq:kernel-weighted-prime-power-gauss}$$ For $j\ge2$, the last bound follows from $(p+1)/(p-1)\le2$ and <a href="#eq:kernel-prime-power-gauss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-prime-power-gauss">[eq:kernel-prime-power-gauss]</a>. For $j=1$, the bound obtained from the formula is $(1+p^{-1/2})/(p-1)$; its ratio to $p^{-1/2}$ is $1/(\sqrt p-1)\le2$. Thus the exponent-one case is included.

<a id="eq:kernel-large-coefficient"></a>
For a fixed divisor $u\mid\operatorname{rad}(q_1)$, the coprime local mean is a product of factors from <a href="#eq:kernel-prime-power-gauss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-prime-power-gauss">[eq:kernel-prime-power-gauss]</a> and <a href="#eq:kernel-weighted-prime-power-gauss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-weighted-prime-power-gauss">[eq:kernel-weighted-prime-power-gauss]</a>. Its modulus is at most $2^{\omega(u)}q_1^{-1/2}$. The factor at primes of $D$ has modulus at most one by <a href="#eq:kernel-root-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-root-law">[eq:kernel-root-law]</a>. Dropping the restriction $u\le H$ after taking absolute values therefore gives $$|c(\alpha_0)|
 \le q_1^{-1/2}\sum_{u\mid\operatorname{rad}(q_1)}2^{\omega(u)}
 =3^{\omega(q_1)}q_1^{-1/2}
 \ll q_1^{-1/3}
 \le H^{-1/3}.
 \label{eq:kernel-large-coefficient}$$ The penultimate implied constant is absolute. For example, one may use $$C_*=
 \prod_{\substack{p<729\\p\text{ odd prime}}}\max(1,3p^{-1/6}),$$ since $3\le p^{1/6}$ for every prime $p\ge729$. Repeated prime powers only increase $q_1^{1/6}$. This argument covers all prime powers in $q_1$, and if $q(\alpha_D)\nmid D$, every coefficient under discussion is already zero by <a href="#eq:kernel-root-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-root-law">[eq:kernel-root-law]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
###### Completion of the uniform comparison.

On the minor arcs, <a href="#eq:kernel-minor-actual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-minor-actual">[eq:kernel-minor-actual]</a> and <a href="#eq:kernel-minor-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-minor-model">[eq:kernel-minor-model]</a> apply. On a major arc, <a href="#eq:kernel-major-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-major-model">[eq:kernel-major-model]</a>, <a href="#eq:kernel-major-actual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-major-actual">[eq:kernel-major-actual]</a>, <a href="#eq:kernel-small-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-small-coefficient">[eq:kernel-small-coefficient]</a> and <a href="#eq:kernel-large-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-large-coefficient">[eq:kernel-large-coefficient]</a> apply, with $|V_N(y)|\le1$. For clarity, every approximation error is bounded here using the fixed value <a href="#eq:kernel-beta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-beta">[eq:kernel-beta]</a>: $$\begin{aligned}
 DHN^{-1/20}\sqrt{\log N}
   &\le N^{-0.049985}\sqrt{\log N},\\
 DH^2N^{-1/10}
   &\le N^{-0.09998},\\
 qD^2H^3/N
   &\le N^{-0.899965},\\
 \frac{qD^2H^2(1+|y|N)}{\sqrt N}
   &\le3N^{-0.29997}.
\end{aligned}$$ In the last two lines $q\le N^{1/10}$ and $|y|\le2N^{-9/10}$. Each bound is $O(N^{-\beta/6})=O(H^{-1/3})$ with an absolute constant. Together with the coefficient bound this proves <a href="#eq:kernel-uniform-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-uniform-approximation">[eq:kernel-uniform-approximation]</a> for all sufficiently large $N$. All size and separation thresholds used above are absolute. For the bounded remaining range, the elementary bounds $\|S\|_\infty\ll DH$ and $\|S_0\|_\infty\le DH^2$ absorb that range by increasing the absolute constant. Thus <a href="#eq:kernel-uniform-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-uniform-approximation">[eq:kernel-uniform-approximation]</a> is uniform in all the data of the proposition.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
###### The directional bilinear identity.

<a id="eq:kernel-plancherel-error"></a>
It remains to pass from the Fourier comparison to the supports in the statement. Extend $F$ and $J$ by zero to $\mathbb Z$. For any finitely supported kernel $h$ define $$\mathcal B_h(F,J)=\frac1N\sum_{x\in\mathbb Z}
                              \sum_{n\in\mathbb Z}h(n)F(x)J(x+n).$$ Both sums use counting measure; the displayed $1/N$ normalizes only the base variable. Let $$\widetilde F(\alpha)=\sum_{x\in\mathbb Z}F(x)e(-x\alpha),
 \qquad
 \widetilde J(\alpha)=\sum_{y\in\mathbb Z}J(y)e(-y\alpha),
 \qquad
 S_h(\alpha)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb Z}h(n)e(\alpha n).$$ Expanding and integrating the characters imposes $y=x+n$, giving the bilinear identity $$\mathcal B_h(F,J)=\frac1N\int_0^1
 S_h(\alpha)\widetilde F(-\alpha)\widetilde J(\alpha)\,d\alpha.$$ There are no conjugates in this identity. Cauchy–Schwarz and counting-measure Plancherel now give $$\begin{aligned}
 |\mathcal B_{k-k_0}(F,J)|
 &\le\frac{\|S-S_0\|_\infty}{N}
          \|F\|_{\ell^2(\mathbb Z)}\|J\|_{\ell^2(\mathbb Z)}
 \ll H^{-1/3},
 \label{eq:kernel-plancherel-error}
\end{aligned}$$ because each of the last two norms is at most $\sqrt N$.

Every term in $\mathcal B_k(F,J)$ contains $F(x)J(x+m^2)$ with $m\ge1$, and this product is zero by hypothesis. Thus $\mathcal B_k(F,J)=0$ term by term, regardless of the signed weights in <a href="#eq:kernel-actual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-actual">[eq:kernel-actual]</a>. For a pair $x,y$ with $F(x)J(y)\ne0$, the ordering assumption gives $1\le y-x\le N-1$, and the residue conditions give $y-x\equiv r\pmod D$. Consequently both indicators in <a href="#eq:kernel-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-model">[eq:kernel-model]</a> are automatic for every contributing pair. Its expansion is exactly $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal B_{k_0}(F,J)
 &=\frac{D}{N^2}\sum_{x,y\in[N]}F(x)J(y)
                    \sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\mathfrak g(\xi)e(-\xi(y-x))\\
 &=D\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\mathfrak g(\xi)
                    \widehat F(-\xi)\widehat J(\xi).
\end{aligned}$$ Combining this with <a href="#eq:kernel-plancherel-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-plancherel-error">[eq:kernel-plancherel-error]</a> proves <a href="#eq:kernel-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-conclusion">[eq:kernel-conclusion]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rem-kernel-interval-normalization"></a>
<div id="rem:kernel-interval-normalization" class="remark">

*Remark 18*. The normalization for later interval restrictions follows directly from the proposition. Suppose $I_1,I_2\subseteq[N]$ are nonempty intervals with $\max I_1<\min I_2$, and let $\Xi$ be the frequency set in <a href="#eq:kernel-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-conclusion">[eq:kernel-conclusion]</a>. Suppose $F_i$ is the indicator of the restriction of a square-difference-free set to $I_i$ and a class $a_i$ modulo $D$, where $a_2-a_1$ is a strict square class. The coefficients $$c_i(\xi)=\frac D{|I_i|}\sum_{n\in I_i}F_i(n)e(-n\xi)
          =\frac{DN}{|I_i|}\widehat F_i(\xi)$$ satisfy $$\left|\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\mathfrak g(\xi)c_1(-\xi)c_2(\xi)\right|
 \ll H^{-1/3}\frac{DN^2}{|I_1||I_2|}.$$ This conclusion uses the integer supports of the restrictions; it requires no pointwise positivity of a Fourier lift.

</div>

<a id="sec:scale-transfer"></a>
### Comparison along square-step progressions

We next compare the product-space functional with the same functional on shorter intervals. Fixing a few prime coordinates makes its low Fourier coefficients exact averages on a residue class. Splitting that class into progressions with square step preserves the forbidden integer differences and permits a change of scale. The comparison proved here uses no induction hypothesis. Restriction to square-step progressions is already a key feature of quantitative square-difference arguments; see the Main Lemma of (Pintz, Steiger, and Szemerédi 1988) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[PSS1988](#ref-PSS1988)] and (Green and Sawhney 2025, Lemma 6.2) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[GreenSawhney2025](#ref-GreenSawhney2025)]. Here it is used to compare the signed multilinear functional itself.

<a id="eq:prime-threshold-conditions"></a>
Keep the constants $\gamma=1/64$ and $\beta=10^{-5}$ fixed as above. Enlarge the fixed prime threshold $P$, if necessary, so that the tuple measures are available for every prime $p\ge P$ and $$\prod_{\substack{p\ge P\\p\ {\rm prime}}}(1+p^{-3})-1\le\frac14,
 \qquad
 |\mathfrak g(\xi)|\le q(\xi)^{-1/3}
 \label{eq:prime-threshold-conditions}$$ whenever $q(\xi)$ is squarefree and all its prime factors are at least $P$. Such a choice is possible: the product converges, while the prime Gauss estimate used in Proposition <a href="#prop:square-kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:square-kernel">17</a> gives $$\left|\frac1{p-1}\sum_{x\in\mathbb F_p^\times}e(ax^2/p)\right|
 \le\frac{\sqrt p+1}{p-1}
 =\frac1{\sqrt p-1}\le p^{-1/3}$$ for all sufficiently large $p$ and all $a\ne0$ in $\mathbb F_p$. Chinese remaindering then gives the second inequality in <a href="#eq:prime-threshold-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-threshold-conditions">[eq:prime-threshold-conditions]</a>, including $q(\xi)=1$. Define the fixed integer <a id="eq:fixed-small-modulus"></a>$$M_0=8\prod_{\substack{3\le p<P\\p\ {\rm prime}}}p.
 \label{eq:fixed-small-modulus}$$ In particular $M_0\ge8$.

<a id="eq:scale-parameters"></a>
<a id="eq:small-residue-input"></a>
For every positive integer $N$, put $$\begin{gathered}
 Q=N^\beta,\qquad H=N^{\beta/2},\qquad
 \mathcal P_N=\{p\ {\rm prime}:P\le p\le Q\},\\
 \tau=\frac{\beta\gamma}{1000(d+1)},\qquad
 \sigma_0=\frac{\gamma\tau}{4}.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:scale-parameters}$$ For a square-difference-free set $A\subseteq[N]$, the quantity that will eventually be estimated by induction is <a id="eq:induction-functional"></a>$$Y(N,A)=
 Z_{\mathcal P_N}\bigl(\mathcal L_{[N],\mathcal P_N,Q}1_A\bigr).
 \label{eq:induction-functional}$$ It is nonnegative by Proposition <a href="#prop:signed-holder" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:signed-holder">11</a>. We also set $$f_s(n)=M_0\,1_{\{n\equiv s\pmod{M_0}\}}1_A(n)
 \quad(s\in\mathbb Z/M_0\mathbb Z),
 \qquad
 1_A=\mathbb E_{s\bmod M_0}f_s.
 \label{eq:small-residue-input}$$ The factor $M_0$ in this definition will be retained in the estimates. All constants below may depend on $P,d,\beta,\gamma$ and the earlier fixed construction parameters.

<a id="stmt-lem-exact-fiber"></a>
<div id="lem:exact-fiber" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:interval-fiber-coefficient"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Exact coefficients on a fiber). *Let $N\ge1$, let $A\subseteq[N]$, and define $f_s$ by <a href="#eq:small-residue-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-residue-input">[eq:small-residue-input]</a>. Let $I\subseteq[N]$ be a nonempty interval, let $B\subseteq\mathcal P_N$, and put $$q_B=\prod_{p\in B}p,\qquad
 \mathcal S=\mathcal P_N\setminus B,\qquad D=M_0q_B.$$ Fix $s\bmod M_0$ and $z_B\in X_B$, and let $a\bmod D$ be their combined residue class. If $$g_I=(\mathcal L_{I,\mathcal P_N,Q}f_s)(z_B,\cdot),$$ then every character $\xi$ on $X_{\mathcal S}$ satisfying $q(\xi)q_B\le Q$ has coefficient $$\widehat g_I(\xi)
 =\frac D{|I|}
   \sum_{\substack{n\in I\\n\equiv a\pmod D}}
       1_A(n)e(-n\xi).
 \label{eq:interval-fiber-coefficient}$$ Here $q_\varnothing=1$, and the hat denotes the normalized Fourier coefficient on the remaining product space.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-exact-fiber"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-exact-fiber</summary>

*Proof.* Write $\chi_\xi(z)=e(z\xi)$ for a product-space character. Each original character has a unique decomposition $\xi+\eta$, where $\xi$ is supported on $\mathcal S$ and $\eta$ on $B$. Their denominators are coprime, so $q(\xi+\eta)=q(\xi)q(\eta)$. The hypothesis ensures that every character $\eta$ on $X_B$ is retained in the lift. Orthogonality over this entire character group gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \widehat g_I(\xi)
 &=\sum_{\eta\ {\rm on}\ X_B}
     \frac1{|I|}\sum_{n\in I}
       f_s(n)e(-n(\xi+\eta))\chi_\eta(z_B)\\
 &=\frac{q_B}{|I|}
     \sum_{\substack{n\in I\\
                     n\equiv z_p\pmod p\ (p\in B)}}
       f_s(n)e(-n\xi).
\end{aligned}$$ Substituting <a href="#eq:small-residue-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-residue-input">[eq:small-residue-input]</a> proves <a href="#eq:interval-fiber-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interval-fiber-coefficient">[eq:interval-fiber-coefficient]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-scale-transfer"></a>
<div id="prop:scale-transfer" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:scale-transfer-diagonal"></a>
<a id="eq:scale-transfer-mixed"></a>
<a id="eq:scale-transfer-factor"></a>
**Proposition 20** (Comparison with a shorter interval). *There are constants $C_{\rm tr}<\infty$ and $N_{\rm tr}$ with the following property. Let $N\ge N_{\rm tr}$, let $A\subseteq[N]$ be square-difference-free, and let $B\subseteq\mathcal P_N$ satisfy $q_B\le N^\tau$. Put $$\mathcal S=\mathcal P_N\setminus B,\qquad
 D=M_0q_B,\qquad N'=\lceil N/D^2\rceil,\qquad
 \lambda=\frac{D^2N'}N.$$ For any $s\bmod M_0$ and $z_B\in X_B$, define $$g=(\mathcal L_{[N],\mathcal P_N,Q}f_s)(z_B,\cdot).$$ Then $1\le N'<N$, and $$Z_{\mathcal S}(g)
 \le \lambda^d
       \max_{\substack{A'\subseteq[N']\\
                       A'\ {\rm square\text{-}difference\text{-}free}}}
                Y(N',A')
       +C_{\rm tr}N^{-\sigma_0}.
 \label{eq:scale-transfer-diagonal}$$ Moreover, for arbitrary choices $s_v\bmod M_0$ and $z_{B,v}\in X_B$, one for every $v\in V$, the functions $$g_v=(\mathcal L_{[N],\mathcal P_N,Q}f_{s_v})(z_{B,v},\cdot)$$ satisfy $$|\mathcal Z_{\mathcal S}((g_v)_{v\in V})|
 \le \lambda^d
       \max_{\substack{A'\subseteq[N']\\
                       A'\ {\rm square\text{-}difference\text{-}free}}}
                Y(N',A')
       +C_{\rm tr}N^{-\sigma_0}.
 \label{eq:scale-transfer-mixed}$$ All constants are uniform in $N,A,B,s,z_B$ and the mixed specifications. Finally, $$1\le\lambda<1+\frac{D^2}{N},\qquad
 \lambda^d=1+O_d(D^2/N)
           =1+O_{d,M_0}(N^{-1+2\tau}).
 \label{eq:scale-transfer-factor}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-scale-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-scale-transfer</summary>

<a id="eq:shorter-scale-lower-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:scale-transfer-cutoffs"></a>
*Proof.* We first record the parameter inequalities that make both lifts available. Since $\tau<\beta/2$, $$Hq_B\le N^{\beta/2+\tau}\le Q.$$ Also $$N'\ge\frac{N}{D^2}\ge M_0^{-2}N^{1-2\tau}.
 \label{eq:shorter-scale-lower-bound}$$ Because $1/2-2\tau>0$, this lower bound is at least $N^{1/2}$ for sufficiently large $N$. As $D\ge8$, we have $N'<N$ for every integer $N\ge2$. Hence, with $Q'=(N')^\beta$, $$H\le Q'\le Q,\qquad
 N\ge10Q^6,\qquad N'\ge10(Q')^6
 \label{eq:scale-transfer-cutoffs}$$ once $N$ is sufficiently large. The length conditions follow from $6\beta<1$ and the uniform growth of $N'$ in <a href="#eq:shorter-scale-lower-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shorter-scale-lower-bound">[eq:shorter-scale-lower-bound]</a>.

<a id="eq:specialized-lift-moments"></a>
*Removing large remaining conductors.* Split $g=g_{\le H}+g_{>H}$ according to the denominator on the remaining prime coordinates $\mathcal S$. Before specialization, these pieces retain exactly the denominator families $$\{U\subseteq\mathcal P_N:q_U\le Q,\ q_{U\setminus B}\le H\},
 \qquad
 \{U\subseteq\mathcal P_N:q_U\le Q,\ q_{U\setminus B}>H\}.$$ Thus Lemma <a href="#lem:lift-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lift-moments">15</a> applies to both pieces. For a function $u$ on $X_B\times X_{\mathcal S}$, uniform counting gives the specialization bound $$\|u(z_B,\cdot)\|_r
 \le q_B^{1/r}\|u\|_r
 \qquad(r\ge1);$$ this is also the estimate in Lemma <a href="#lem:lift-specialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lift-specialization">16</a>. Since $|f_s|\le M_0$, it follows that, for $g_*\in\{g,g_{\le H},g_{>H}\}$ and every fixed even $r$, $$\|g_*\|_r\le M_0q_B^{1/r}N^{o(1)}.
 \label{eq:specialized-lift-moments}$$ All subpower bounds in this proof are uniform over the indicated sets and fibers.

<a id="eq:scale-transfer-first-error"></a>
Telescope the $d$ inputs in the diagonal integral from $g$ to $g_{\le H}$. Each difference has one input $g_{>H}$. Lemma <a href="#lem:conductor-decay" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conductor-decay">13</a> and <a href="#eq:specialized-lift-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:specialized-lift-moments">[eq:specialized-lift-moments]</a> imply, for every fixed $\varepsilon>0$, $$|Z_{\mathcal S}(g)-Z_{\mathcal S}(g_{\le H})|
 \le C_\varepsilon H^{-\gamma}q_BN^\varepsilon.
 \label{eq:scale-transfer-first-error}$$ Indeed the specialization powers in that lemma add to $$\frac12+\frac{d-1}{2(d-1)}=1.$$ The factors $d$ and $M_0^d$ are fixed and are included in $C_\varepsilon$. Applying the moment lemma with sufficiently small individual exponents makes the aggregate moment loss at most $N^\varepsilon$.

<a id="eq:scale-transfer-fiber"></a>
*Splitting the exact fiber into square-step progressions.* Let $a\bmod D$ combine $s$ and $z_B$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:exact-fiber" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:exact-fiber">19</a>, for every remaining $\xi$ with $q(\xi)\le H$, $$\widehat g(\xi)=\frac D N
   \sum_{\substack{n\le N\\n\equiv a\pmod D}}1_A(n)e(-n\xi).
 \label{eq:scale-transfer-fiber}$$ Let $$\begin{gathered}
 J_a=\{j\in[D^2]:j\equiv a\pmod D\},\qquad
 c_j=j-D^2,\\
 A'_j=\{n'\in[N']:D^2n'+c_j\in A\}.
 \end{gathered}$$ There are exactly $D$ elements of $J_a$. The map $(n',j)\mapsto D^2(n'-1)+j$ bijects $[N']\times[D^2]$ with $[D^2N']$. Restricting $j$ to $J_a$ selects exactly the residue class $a\bmod D$. The definition of $A'_j$ assigns zero to all padded points greater than $N$, so this decomposition incurs no additive error.

Each $A'_j$ is square-difference-free: a difference $m^2>0$ between its elements would give the difference $D^2m^2=(Dm)^2$ between the corresponding elements of $A$. This argument concerns actual integer squares.

For $p\in\mathcal S$, the integer $D$ is invertible modulo $p$. Define $$T_j(z)_p=D^{-2}(z_p-c_j)\pmod p,\qquad
 h_j^H=\mathcal L_{[N'],\mathcal S,H}1_{A'_j}.$$ Each $T_j$ is a translation followed by multiplication by the nonzero square $(D^{-1})^2$. Multiplication of a remaining frequency by $D^2$ preserves its exact denominator, so changing variables $\eta=D^2\xi$ in the Fourier expansion of <a href="#eq:scale-transfer-fiber" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scale-transfer-fiber">[eq:scale-transfer-fiber]</a> gives the exact identity <a id="eq:scale-transfer-affine-average"></a>$$g_{\le H}(z)
 =\frac{\lambda}{D}\sum_{j\in J_a}h_j^H(T_j(z)).
 \label{eq:scale-transfer-affine-average}$$ For clarity, the coefficient of $e(z\xi)$ on the right is $$\frac{DN'}N\sum_{j\in J_a}e(-c_j\xi)
 \left(\frac1{N'}\sum_{n'\le N'}
       1_{A'_j}(n')e(-D^2n'\xi)\right),$$ which is exactly <a href="#eq:scale-transfer-fiber" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scale-transfer-fiber">[eq:scale-transfer-fiber]</a>.

<a id="eq:scale-transfer-progressions"></a>
The seminorm triangle inequality and affine invariance in Lemma <a href="#lem:functional-properties" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:functional-properties">12</a> now imply $$Z_{\mathcal S}(g_{\le H})^{1/d}
 \le \frac{\lambda}{D}
        \sum_{j\in J_a}Z_{\mathcal S}(h_j^H)^{1/d}
 \le \lambda\max_{j\in J_a}Z_{\mathcal S}(h_j^H)^{1/d}.$$ Consequently, $$Z_{\mathcal S}(g_{\le H})
 \le\lambda^d\max_{j\in J_a}Z_{\mathcal S}(h_j^H).
 \label{eq:scale-transfer-progressions}$$ The averaging by $D$ is essential: the number of progressions causes no further loss.

<a id="eq:scale-transfer-second-error"></a>
*Recovering the smaller-scale functional.* Set $$h_j^{Q'}=\mathcal L_{[N'],\mathcal S,Q'}1_{A'_j}.$$ The difference $h_j^{Q'}-h_j^H$ has conductor greater than $H$. By <a href="#eq:scale-transfer-cutoffs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scale-transfer-cutoffs">[eq:scale-transfer-cutoffs]</a>, the moment lemma applies at scale $N'$ to these lifts and their pieces. A second telescoping application of Lemma <a href="#lem:conductor-decay" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conductor-decay">13</a>, this time without specialization, gives $$|Z_{\mathcal S}(h_j^H)-Z_{\mathcal S}(h_j^{Q'})|
 \le C_\varepsilon H^{-\gamma}(N')^\varepsilon
 \label{eq:scale-transfer-second-error}$$ uniformly in $j$.

<a id="eq:scale-transfer-projection"></a>
The lift $h_j^{Q'}$ depends only on coordinates in $\mathcal S'=\mathcal P_{N'}\setminus B$, because every prime in a denominator at most $Q'$ is itself at most $Q'$. On the other hand, the full lift $$u'_j=\mathcal L_{[N'],\mathcal P_{N'},Q'}1_{A'_j}$$ becomes exactly $h_j^{Q'}$ after uniform averaging over $B\cap\mathcal P_{N'}$. Character orthogonality removes precisely the denominators containing one of those primes. By Lemma <a href="#lem:functional-properties" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:functional-properties">12</a>, coordinate averaging contracts the seminorm; unused probability factors integrate to one. Therefore $$Z_{\mathcal S}(h_j^{Q'})
 =Z_{\mathcal S'}(h_j^{Q'})
 \le Z_{\mathcal P_{N'}}(u'_j)
 =Y(N',A'_j).
 \label{eq:scale-transfer-projection}$$ This also covers primes of $B$ larger than $Q'$, which are absent from $\mathcal P_{N'}$ already.

<a id="eq:scale-transfer-explicit-error"></a>
Combining <a href="#eq:scale-transfer-first-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scale-transfer-first-error">[eq:scale-transfer-first-error]</a>, <a href="#eq:scale-transfer-progressions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scale-transfer-progressions">[eq:scale-transfer-progressions]</a>, <a href="#eq:scale-transfer-second-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scale-transfer-second-error">[eq:scale-transfer-second-error]</a>, and <a href="#eq:scale-transfer-projection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scale-transfer-projection">[eq:scale-transfer-projection]</a> proves <a href="#eq:scale-transfer-diagonal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scale-transfer-diagonal">[eq:scale-transfer-diagonal]</a> with error $$C_\varepsilon\left(
 H^{-\gamma}q_BN^\varepsilon+
 \lambda^dH^{-\gamma}(N')^\varepsilon\right).
 \label{eq:scale-transfer-explicit-error}$$

*Uniform error bounds and mixed inputs.* The definition of $N'$ gives $1\le\lambda<1+D^2/N$, and $$D^2/N\le M_0^2N^{-1+2\tau}=o(1).$$ Since $d$ is fixed, these inequalities prove <a href="#eq:scale-transfer-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scale-transfer-factor">[eq:scale-transfer-factor]</a> and show that $\lambda^d$ is bounded for large $N$. Choose $\varepsilon=\beta\gamma/8$ in <a href="#eq:scale-transfer-explicit-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scale-transfer-explicit-error">[eq:scale-transfer-explicit-error]</a>. Its two terms, before the bounded factor $\lambda^d$, are at most $$N^{-3\beta\gamma/8+\tau}
 \quad\hbox{and}\quad N^{-3\beta\gamma/8},$$ respectively. As $\tau\le\beta\gamma/1000$ and $\sigma_0=\gamma\tau/4\le\tau/4$, they are $O(N^{-\sigma_0})$. This establishes the diagonal bound with constants independent of any inductive estimate for $Y$.

Every $g_v$ in the mixed statement is real and satisfies the same diagonal bound. Its right-hand side is nonnegative and independent of $v$. Proposition <a href="#prop:signed-holder" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:signed-holder">11</a> therefore gives <a href="#eq:scale-transfer-mixed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scale-transfer-mixed">[eq:scale-transfer-mixed]</a>. ◻

</details>

In particular, the mixed estimate holds pointwise after specifying all labels at primes in $B$, regardless of the law used to choose those labels. It can therefore be integrated against any such probability law. When the prime laws form a product, the remaining coordinates still carry the full laws used in Proposition <a href="#prop:scale-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:scale-transfer">20</a>. No reflection positivity is required of the law on the specified labels.

<a id="sec:ordered-contraction"></a>
### Contraction on ordered intervals

<a id="eq:good-residue-proportion"></a>
A tuple of residue classes $\boldsymbol s=(s_v)_{v\in V}\in(\mathbb Z/M_0\mathbb Z)^V$ is called *good* if $$s_{v_{j+1}}-s_{v_j}
 \quad\text{is a strict square class modulo }M_0
 \qquad(0\le j<24),$$ where $v_{24}=v_0$. Thus each difference is $1$ modulo $8$ and a nonzero square at every odd prime dividing $M_0$. Let $$\rho=M_0^{-d}\bigl|\{\boldsymbol s\in(\mathbb Z/M_0\mathbb Z)^V:
                         \boldsymbol s\text{ is good}\}\bigr|.
 \label{eq:good-residue-proportion}$$ We will bound the contribution of each good tuple when every prime uses the nonexceptional part of its measure. First we show that these assignments have a fixed positive proportion; the proof also explains the cycle length $24$. We retain the parameters and lifts from the preceding section.

<a id="stmt-lem-good-residues"></a>
<div id="lem:good-residues" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21** (Existence of good residue assignments). *The proportion $\rho$ defined in <a href="#eq:good-residue-proportion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:good-residue-proportion">[eq:good-residue-proportion]</a> satisfies $$\rho\ge M_0^{-23}>0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-good-residues"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-good-residues</summary>

*Proof.* We construct a length-$24$ sequence of admissible increments with sum zero at each factor of $M_0$. Modulo $8$, take all the increments equal to $1$; their sum is $24\equiv0\pmod8$.

Let $p<P$ be an odd prime. If $-1$ is a square modulo $p$, repeat the pair $(1,-1)$ twelve times. Both entries are nonzero squares and each pair has sum zero. If $-1$ is not a square, put $$S=\{x^2:x\in\mathbb F_p\}.$$ The sets $S$ and $-1-S$ each have $(p+1)/2$ elements, so they intersect. Thus $-1=a+b$ for some $a,b\in S$. Neither $a$ nor $b$ is zero, since otherwise $-1$ would be a square. The triple $(a,b,1)$ consists of nonzero squares and has sum zero. Repeat this triple eight times. This also covers $p=3$, where the triple is $(1,1,1)$.

The Chinese remainder theorem now gives increments $\Delta_0,\ldots,\Delta_{23}\pmod{M_0}$, each a strict square class, with $\sum_{j=0}^{23}\Delta_j=0$. For each initial residue $a\pmod{M_0}$, prescribe $$s_{v_0}=a,\qquad
 s_{v_j}=a+\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}\Delta_i\quad(1\le j<24).$$ The last edge has increment $\Delta_{23}$ because the sum of all the increments is zero. The slots $v_0,\ldots,v_{23}$ are distinct, since their block words are the distinct cyclic shifts of the identity word. Repetition of residue values at nonadjacent slots causes no difficulty. The other $d-24$ residues are unrestricted. We have therefore exhibited at least $M_0\cdot M_0^{d-24}$ good assignments among the $M_0^d$ possible assignments, which proves the bound. ◻

</details>

The length $24$ is divisible by $8$, $2$, and $3$, exactly the three local cycle lengths used in this construction. Thus this choice works uniformly for every prime included in the fixed modulus.

We now prove the analytic estimate for each good assignment.

<a id="stmt-prop-nonexceptional-contraction"></a>
<div id="prop:nonexceptional-contraction" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:nonexceptional-contraction"></a>
**Proposition 22** (Contraction for good residue assignments). *There are absolute constants $C$ and $N_0$ such that, whenever $N\ge N_0$, $A\subseteq[N]$ has no positive square difference, and $\boldsymbol s$ is good, one has $$\left|\int\prod_{v\in V}
  \mathcal L_{[N],\mathcal P_N,Q}f_{s_v}(z_v)
  \,d\bigotimes_{p\in\mathcal P_N}(\mu_p-\varepsilon_p)\right|
 \le C N^{-\sigma_0}.
 \label{eq:nonexceptional-contraction}$$ The constants are uniform in $A$ and $\boldsymbol s$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-nonexceptional-contraction"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-nonexceptional-contraction</summary>

*Proof.* Write $\eta_p$ for the total mass of $\varepsilon_p$, and set $$\mu_p^\circ=\frac{\mu_p-\varepsilon_p}{1-\eta_p},
 \qquad \Lambda^\circ=\bigotimes_{p\in\mathcal P_N}\mu_p^\circ.$$ It suffices to prove the assertion with $\Lambda^\circ$ in place of the measure in <a href="#eq:nonexceptional-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:nonexceptional-contraction">[eq:nonexceptional-contraction]</a>, since the latter is $\prod_p(1-\eta_p)\Lambda^\circ$ and $0\le\prod_p(1-\eta_p)\le1$. The single-slot marginals of $\Lambda^\circ$ are uniform. Moreover, at each prime its marginal on any directed cycle edge is uniform on strict square pairs. We use conductor decay for $\Lambda^\circ$, without any claim that this normalized measure is reflection positive.

*Selecting an ordered pair.* Put $K=\lfloor N^\tau\rfloor$, and partition $[N]$ into ordered consecutive intervals $I_1,\ldots,I_K$ of sizes differing by at most one. Writing $w_i=|I_i|/N$, we have the exact identity $$\mathcal L_{[N],\mathcal P_N,Q}f_s
   =\sum_{i=1}^K w_i\mathcal L_{I_i,\mathcal P_N,Q}f_s.$$ Thus the integral is an average over independent interval choices in all slots, with probabilities $w_i$. For large $N$, every interval has length at least $\tfrac12N^{1-\tau}$. Since $1-\tau>6\beta$, this length is at least $10Q^6$. Lemma <a href="#lem:lift-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lift-moments">15</a> therefore applies uniformly to every interval lift and every exact-denominator truncation used below. Its application to $f_s$, which is bounded by the fixed constant $M_0$, changes only the constants. All occurrences of $N^{o(1)}$ in this proof are uniform over these interval choices, residues and sets.

First consider assignments for which all 24 cycle slots choose the same interval. Their total probability is $$\sum_{i=1}^K w_i^{24}
 \le(\max_i w_i)^{23}\ll N^{-23\tau}.$$ For every assignment, ordinary Hölder and the uniform single-slot marginals give $$\left|\int\prod_v G_v(z_v)\,d\Lambda^\circ\right|
 \le\prod_v\|G_v\|_d=N^{o(1)},
 \qquad
 G_v=\mathcal L_{I(v),\mathcal P_N,Q}f_{s_v}.$$ Here $d$ is even, so the required moment is supplied directly by Lemma <a href="#lem:lift-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lift-moments">15</a>. The contribution of these assignments is therefore $O(N^{-23\tau+o(1)})$; there is no restriction on the interval choices in the other slots.

In every remaining assignment there is a directed cycle edge $u\to v$ whose first interval precedes its second. Indeed, if the cyclic sequence of interval indices had no ascent, it would be nonincreasing all the way around the cycle, and hence constant. Fix one ascending edge, and denote its two intervals by $I_1,I_2$ for the rest of this argument. All integers in $I_1$ precede all integers in $I_2$.

<a id="eq:interval-truncation-error"></a>
*Eliminating the other slots.* Retain these two lifts in full. In each of the other $d-2$ slots, truncate the lift to exact conductors at most $T=N^\tau$. Lemma <a href="#lem:conductor-decay" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conductor-decay">13</a>, applied during a telescoping replacement of these slots, bounds the total error by $$O(T^{-\gamma}N^{o(1)})=O(N^{-\gamma\tau+o(1)}).
 \label{eq:interval-truncation-error}$$ Every norm used here is controlled by Lemma <a href="#lem:lift-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lift-moments">15</a>: the retained and discarded pieces are subfamilies of complete exact denominator pieces. This error is estimated before the Fourier expansion that follows.

<a id="eq:interval-fourier-cost"></a>
Each Fourier coefficient in a truncated slot has modulus at most $M_0$, and there are at most $\sum_{q\le T}\varphi(q)\ll T^2$ frequencies. The product expansion of all other slots consequently has total coefficient absolute sum at most $$C_{d,M_0}T^{2(d-2)}\ll N^{2d\tau}.
 \label{eq:interval-fourier-cost}$$ For one term in this expansion, let $B\subseteq\mathcal P_N$ be the union of the prime supports of its characters, and put $$R=\prod_{p\in B}p.$$ The conductors are squarefree, so their least common multiple satisfies <a id="eq:interval-mode-modulus"></a>$$R\le T^{d-2}\le N^{d\tau}.
 \label{eq:interval-mode-modulus}$$

Condition on all slot labels at the primes in $B$. The expanded characters become constants of modulus one. Since $\Lambda^\circ$ is a product over primes, the tuple laws at the remaining primes are unchanged and independent. The two selected labels now have fixed coordinates $a_{1,B},a_{2,B}$ at $B$, whose difference is a nonzero square at each prime in $B$. At every remaining prime their pair marginal is still uniform on strict square pairs.

<a id="eq:interval-pair-fourier"></a>
Let $\mathcal S=\mathcal P_N\setminus B$, let $s_1=s_u$ and $s_2=s_v$, and define functions on $X_{\mathcal S}$ by $$g_i(z)=\bigl(\mathcal L_{I_i,\mathcal P_N,Q}f_{s_i}\bigr)(a_{i,B},z),
 \qquad i=1,2.$$ With Fourier coefficients normalized for uniform measure on $X_{\mathcal S}$, their conditional pair integral is exactly $$\sum_{\xi\text{ on }X_{\mathcal S}}
   \widehat g_1(-\xi)\widehat g_2(\xi)\mathfrak g(\xi).
 \label{eq:interval-pair-fourier}$$ To see this, simultaneous translation kills character pairs whose frequencies do not sum to zero. For a matching pair, the multiplier is the character average of the difference of the labels. At every odd prime a uniform nonzero square is the square of a uniform nonzero unit. The Chinese remainder theorem therefore identifies this average with the unit-square multiplier $\mathfrak g(\xi)$ used in Proposition <a href="#prop:square-kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:square-kernel">17</a>.

<a id="eq:interval-pair-tail"></a>
*Estimating the selected pair.* Specialization on a uniform coordinate block of size $R$ costs at most $R^{1/2}$ in $L^2$: for every function $G$, $$\|G(a_B,\cdot)\|_2^2\le R\|G\|_2^2.$$ This inequality holds at every specified fiber, independently of the law under which that fiber was selected. Hence $\|g_i\|_2\le R^{1/2}N^{o(1)}$. By the bound $|\mathfrak g(\xi)|\le q(\xi)^{-1/3}$, Parseval and Cauchy–Schwarz, the part of <a href="#eq:interval-pair-fourier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interval-pair-fourier">[eq:interval-pair-fourier]</a> with conductor exceeding $H$ has modulus at most $$H^{-1/3}\|g_1\|_2\|g_2\|_2
 \le H^{-1/3}R N^{o(1)}.
 \label{eq:interval-pair-tail}$$

<a id="eq:interval-selected-fiber"></a>
We next identify the remaining coefficients with actual integer fibers. Put $D=M_0R$. The parameter inequalities <a id="eq:interval-parameter-room"></a>$$d\tau<\frac{\beta\gamma}{1000}<\frac\beta2
 \label{eq:interval-parameter-room}$$ imply $RH\le Q$ and, for sufficiently large $N$, $D\le N^\beta$. For a remaining frequency $\xi$ with $q(\xi)\le H$, all frequencies $\xi+\eta$, where $\eta$ ranges over the character group on $B$, therefore occur in the original lift. Summing this entire character group gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \widehat g_i(\xi)
 &=\sum_{\eta\text{ on }B}\frac1{|I_i|}
     \sum_{n\in I_i}f_{s_i}(n)e(-n(\xi+\eta))e(a_{i,B}\eta)\notag\\
 &=\frac{D}{|I_i|}
     \sum_{\substack{n\in I_i\\n\equiv a_i\pmod D}}
           1_A(n)e(-n\xi),
 \label{eq:interval-selected-fiber}
\end{aligned}$$ where $a_i\pmod D$ combines $s_i\pmod {M_0}$ and the coordinates $a_{i,B}$ by the Chinese remainder theorem. Character orthogonality supplies the factor $R$, while the definition of $f_{s_i}$ supplies $M_0$. Goodness of $\boldsymbol s$ and the constraints at the conditioned primes show that $a_2-a_1$ is a strict square class modulo $D$.

The remaining frequencies of conductor at most $H$ are precisely the frequencies with squarefree conductor at most $H$ coprime to $D$. Indeed, $D$ contains every prime less than $P$; every prime divisor of such a conductor is consequently an available prime at least $P$, at most $H\le Q$, and outside $B$. This includes the zero frequency under the convention $q(0)=1$.

<a id="eq:interval-kernel-cost"></a>
Define functions on $[N]$ by $$F_i(n)=1_A(n)1_{I_i}(n)1_{n\equiv a_i\pmod D}.$$ They are bounded by one, their supports are ordered, and there is no positive square difference from the first support to the second. Moreover, $D$ is eight times an odd squarefree number and $D\le N^\beta$. All hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:square-kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:square-kernel">17</a> hold. If the Fourier coefficients of $F_i$ use normalization $N^{-1}$, then <a href="#eq:interval-selected-fiber" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interval-selected-fiber">[eq:interval-selected-fiber]</a> gives $\widehat g_i(\xi)=DN\widehat F_i(\xi)/|I_i|$. Thus the part of <a href="#eq:interval-pair-fourier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interval-pair-fourier">[eq:interval-pair-fourier]</a> of conductor at most $H$ is bounded in modulus by $$\begin{aligned}
 &\frac{D^2N^2}{|I_1||I_2|}
 \left|\sum_{\substack{q(\xi)\le H,\ q(\xi)\text{ squarefree}\\
                       (q(\xi),D)=1}}
      \mathfrak g(\xi)\widehat F_1(-\xi)\widehat F_2(\xi)\right|
 \notag\\
 &\hspace{2cm}\ll H^{-1/3}D\frac{N^2}{|I_1||I_2|}
 \ll H^{-1/3}D N^{2\tau}.
 \label{eq:interval-kernel-cost}
\end{aligned}$$ The factor $D$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:square-kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:square-kernel">17</a> has canceled one of the two fiber-normalization factors.

<a id="eq:interval-three-errors"></a>
Combining <a href="#eq:interval-pair-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interval-pair-tail">[eq:interval-pair-tail]</a> and <a href="#eq:interval-kernel-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interval-kernel-cost">[eq:interval-kernel-cost]</a> bounds each conditional pair integral by $O(N^{-\beta/6+(d+2)\tau+o(1)})$. This is uniform over all conditionings of positive probability. Averaging them, and then summing the Fourier expansion using <a href="#eq:interval-fourier-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interval-fourier-cost">[eq:interval-fourier-cost]</a>, bounds the truncated integral for each unequal-cycle interval assignment by $$O\bigl(N^{-\beta/6+(3d+2)\tau+o(1)}\bigr).$$ Averaging interval assignments incurs no further loss. Including the equal-cycle contribution and <a href="#eq:interval-truncation-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interval-truncation-error">[eq:interval-truncation-error]</a>, the original integral has modulus at most $$C\left(N^{-23\tau+o(1)}+N^{-\gamma\tau+o(1)}
       +N^{-\beta/6+(3d+2)\tau+o(1)}\right).
 \label{eq:interval-three-errors}$$ Finally, $23\tau=5888\sigma_0$, $\gamma\tau=4\sigma_0$, and $$\frac\beta6-(3d+2)\tau
 >\beta\left(\frac16-\frac3{64000}\right)
 >\frac\beta7>2\sigma_0.$$ The finitely many uniform moment losses in each term can be bounded by $N^{\sigma_0}$, after increasing the absolute threshold in $N$. Equation <a href="#eq:interval-three-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interval-three-errors">[eq:interval-three-errors]</a> is consequently $O(N^{-\sigma_0})$, as required. All estimates used absolute values of signed Fourier lifts; no pointwise positivity of a lift was needed. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:induction"></a>
### Exceptional terms and the power-saving induction

We now combine the scale comparison, which applies to every small-residue assignment, with the cancellation obtained for good assignments. For good assignments the nonexceptional contribution is already negligible; we control the exceptional parts by expanding the prime measures. The other assignments are bounded directly by the scale comparison. Expanding measures rather than comparing integrands pointwise keeps the argument valid for signed Fourier lifts throughout.

The finite set $V$ and the constants $P,M_0,\beta,\gamma,\tau,\sigma_0$ have now been fixed. Lemma <a href="#lem:good-residues" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:good-residues">21</a> has also established the fixed positive proportion $\rho$ of good small-residue assignments. The prime threshold $P$ was chosen so that <a id="eq:exceptional-euler-budget"></a>$$\prod_{p\ge P}(1+p^{-3})-1\le\frac14.
 \label{eq:exceptional-euler-budget}$$ For every positive integer $n$, define <a id="eq:maximal-functional"></a>$$\mathcal Y(n)=
 \max_{\substack{A\subseteq[n]\\ A\text{ square-difference-free}}}Y(n,A).
 \label{eq:maximal-functional}$$ This is a finite nonnegative number, by Proposition <a href="#prop:signed-holder" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:signed-holder">11</a>. Write $\eta_p=\varepsilon_p(\mathbb F_p^V)$ for the mass of the exceptional measure, so $0\le \eta_p\le p^{-4}$. For $B\subseteq\mathcal P_N$, put <a id="eq:exceptional-subset-notation"></a>$$q_B=\prod_{p\in B}p,\qquad
 \eta_B=\prod_{p\in B}\eta_p,\qquad
 n_B=\left\lceil\frac{N}{(M_0q_B)^2}\right\rceil.
 \label{eq:exceptional-subset-notation}$$ Empty products have value $1$, so $q_\varnothing=\eta_\varnothing=1$ and $n_\varnothing=\lceil N/M_0^2\rceil$.

<a id="stmt-prop-induction-recurrence"></a>
<div id="prop:induction-recurrence" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:functional-recurrence"></a>
**Proposition 23**. *There are absolute constants $K<\infty$ and $N_1\ge2$ such that, for every integer $N\ge N_1$, <a id="eq:uniform-ceiling-error"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal Y(N)
 &\le K N^{-\sigma_0}
 +(1+u_N)\left((1-\rho)\mathcal Y(n_\varnothing)
       +\rho\!
       \sum_{\substack{\varnothing\ne B\subseteq\mathcal P_N\\q_B\le N^\tau}}
            \eta_B\mathcal Y(n_B)\right),
 \label{eq:functional-recurrence}\\
 u_N&=(1+M_0^2N^{2\tau-1})^d-1.
 \label{eq:uniform-ceiling-error}
\end{aligned}$$ In particular, $u_N\to0$ as $N\to\infty$. The constants $K,N_1$ do not depend on any proposed induction bound for $\mathcal Y$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-induction-recurrence"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-induction-recurrence</summary>

<a id="eq:residue-average-functional"></a>
*Proof.* Fix a square-difference-free set $A\subseteq[N]$. Write $$G_s=\mathcal L_{[N],\mathcal P_N,Q}f_s,
 \qquad
 \Phi_{\mathbf s}((z_v)_{v\in V})=\prod_{v\in V}G_{s_v}(z_v),$$ where $\mathbf s=(s_v)_{v\in V}$ is a small-residue assignment. By <a href="#eq:small-residue-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-residue-input">[eq:small-residue-input]</a>, $1_A=\mathbb E_s f_s$. The lift is linear, and the product-space integral is multilinear, so $$Y(N,A)=\mathbb E_{\mathbf s}
       \int\Phi_{\mathbf s}\,d\bigotimes_{p\in\mathcal P_N}\mu_p.
